-homogeneous for all tuples of length
\leq 2f(n)
.

Then any
CPT
-program that distinguishes
{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}
and
{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S^{\prime}}
for all
n\in\bbN
must activate on input
{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}
a h.f. set
x
whose smallest support has size at least
\Omega(f(n))
.

A structure
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
is
{\cal C}^{\textbf{tw}_{n}}
-
homogeneous
if whenever two tuples
\overline{a}
and
\overline{b}
have the same
{\cal C}^{\textbf{tw}_{n}}
-type in
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
, then there is an automorphism of
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
that maps
\overline{a}
to
\overline{b}
. The
{\cal C}^{\textbf{tw}_{n}}
-type of a tuple
\overline{a}
in
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
is the collection of all
{\cal C}^{\textbf{tw}_{n}}
-formulas that are true in
({\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n},\overline{a})
. By closer inspection of the entire proof in
[
10
]
, one can see that homogeneity is actually only required in the weaker sense that for all tuples of some
bounded length
, the
{\cal C}^{\textbf{tw}_{n}}
-type partition coincides with the orbit partition – hence the explicit restriction in the above theorem.

The homogeneity condition is satisfied by certain ordered CFI-graphs, as stated in
[
10
]
and proved in
[
31
]
, and as we will show, the unordered CFI-graphs over hypercubes, which we use for the lower bound in Theorem
2
, satisfy it as well. Therefore, the homogeneity condition is not really a restriction in the cases that are of interest for us, which is why we omitted it in Theorem
1
. What we also omitted is the fact that we have to relate two different notions of support. Theorem
21
refers to the minimum CFI-support of the h.f. sets, whereas the support lower bound above refers to the minimum
\mathbf{Aut}({\mathfrak{G}}^{S})
-support. Therefore, in order to formulate Theorem
1
correctly with all details, we have to speak about the ratio between these two supports.

Definition 30

(CFI-support gap)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a base graph and
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}
a CFI-graph over it. Let
\mu\in\text{HF}(\widehat{E})
. Denote by
s(\mu)
the size of the smallest
\mathbf{Aut}({\mathfrak{G}}^{S})
-support of
\mu
(while
\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)
still denotes the smallest CFI-support).

Then we call the ratio

\alpha(\mu)=\frac{s(\mu)}{|\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)|}

the
CFI-support gap
of
\mu
(with respect to
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}
).

Then the detailed version of Theorem
1
reads as follows:

Theorem 31
.

Let
(G_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n}))_{n\in\bbN}
be a sequence of base graphs. Let
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
be a CFI-graph over
G_{n}
, let
\textbf{tw}_{n}
denote the treewidth of
G_{n}
. Let
f(n)\in{\cal O}(\textbf{tw}_{n})
be a function such that every
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
is
{\cal C}^{\textbf{tw}_{n}}
-homogeneous, for all tuples of length
\leq 2f(n)
.
Let
g(n)
be a function such that the
CFI-support-gap
for every
\mu\in\text{HF}(\widehat{E}_{n})
with minimum support
s(\mu)\in\Omega(f(n))
is bounded by
g(n)
.

If there exists a
CFI-symmetric

CPT
-program
\Pi
that decides the CFI-query on all
{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}
, then for every
G_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n})
, there exists an XOR-circuit
C_{n}
over
G_{n}
that satisfies the following “instantiated properties” from Theorem
21
:

1.

The number of gates in
C_{n}
is polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}|
.

2.

The orbit-size
|\mathbf{Orb}_{G_{n}}(C_{n})|
of the circuit is polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}|
.

3.

C_{n}
is
sensitive
to
\Omega(f(n)/g(n))
many edges in
E_{n}
.

4.

The
fan-in dimension
of
C_{n}
,
restricted to the space

\mathbf{Aut}_{\text{CFI}}({\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S})
, is
{\cal O}(\log|{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}|)
.

5.

If the program
\Pi
is
super-symmetric
in addition to being CFI-symmetric, or if the base graph
G_{n}
decomposes into at most
{\cal O}(\sqrt{\log|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|})
many components when any
f(n)/g(n)
many edges are removed, then also the (unrestricted)
fan-in dimension
of
C_{n}
is
{\cal O}(\log|{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}|)
.

Proof.

Assume such a CPT-program
\Pi
exists. Let
\mu_{n}\in\text{HF}(\widehat{E}_{n})
denote the CFI-symmetric h.f. set with large support that
\Pi
activates on input
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
. Then by Theorem
29
, the smallest
\mathbf{Aut}({\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n})
-support of the object
\mu_{n}
has size
\Omega(f(n))
. Since the CFI-support gap of
\mu_{n}
in
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
is at most
g(n)
, the size of the smallest CFI-support of
\mu_{n}
is at least:
\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu_{n})\in\Omega(f(n)/g(n))
. Theorem
21
applied to
\mu_{n}
yields the XOR-circuit
C_{n}
. Property 3 from Theorem
21
in combination with the bound
\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu_{n})\in\Omega(f(n)/g(n))
means that
C_{n}
is sensitive to
\Omega(f(n)/g(n))
many edges in
E_{n}
.
Property 4 from Theorem
21
bounds the fan-in dimension and the restricted fan-in dimension in terms of
\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu_{n}))
and
\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{\text{CFI}}((\mu_{n}))
, respectively. Lemma
8
states that
\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu_{n})\leq|\mathbf{Orb}_{E}(\mu_{n})|\cdot|\text{tc}(\mu_{n})|
and
\mathbf{maxOrb}_{\text{CFI}}(\mu_{n})\leq|\mathbf{Orb}_{\text{CFI}}(\mu_{n})|\cdot|\text{tc}(\mu_{n})|
. Because
\mu_{n}
is defined by the CPT-program
\Pi
on input
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
, both
|\text{tc}(\mu_{n})|
and
|\mathbf{Orb}_{\text{CFI}}(\mu_{n})|
are polynomially bounded in
|{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}|
(the orbit is bounded because
\mathbf{Aut}_{\text{CFI}}({\mathfrak{G}}^{S})
is a subgroup of
\mathbf{Aut}({\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n})
). This yields a polynomial bound on
\mathbf{maxOrb}_{\text{CFI}}(\mu_{n})
. Together with the
\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{\text{CFI}}((\mu_{n}))
-bound on the restricted fan-in dimension, this gives us Property 4 from this theorem.

Property 5 follows then with Lemma
10
if
\Pi
is super-symmetric, and with Corollary
15
in case that the base graph
G_{n}
splits into a bounded number of components when
\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu_{n})
is removed from it.
∎

6
XOR-circuits for more general hereditarily finite sets

So far, we have shown that
CFI-symmetric
h.f. sets over CFI-structures
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}
can be quite easily transformed into XOR-circuits by factoring out the orbits under the edge-flip-group
\mathbf{Aut}_{\text{CFI}}({\mathfrak{G}})
. Importantly, this construction automatically translates the relevant properties of the h.f. set, such as support size and symmetry, into more or less natural circuit-properties. As a consequence, we can – in principle – limit the power of
CFI-symmetric
algorithms for the CFI-query by proving appropriate lower bounds for certain families of polynomial size symmetric XOR-circuits. Even though all currently known choiceless algorithms for the CFI-query
are
CFI-symmetric, and it is not clear that non-CFI-symmetric algorithms are really more powerful, it would be much nicer if the circuit-translation were so general that it could be used to separate all of CPT from P, and not only the CFI-symmetric algorithms. In this subsection we explore to what extent the circuit construction can be generalised in that direction. We will present a modification of the construction above, that uses additional gadgets, works without the restriction to CFI-symmetric sets, and has almost all properties from Theorem
21
. By “almost all” we mean that the additional gadgets we have to introduce in the circuit are of unknown size. Hence, we cannot be sure that the constructed circuit is always of polynomial size. However, we can formulate a condition on the h.f. sets, which generalises that of CFI-symmetry and guarantees polynomial size of the circuit. This condition concerns Boolean vector spaces with a permutation group acting on the index set. If certain subspaces of
\bbF_{2}^{E}
, which appear as stabiliser groups of the connected components of the sets in
\mu
possess a basis that is (almost) invariant under the permutation group (which will be a subgroup of
\mathbf{Aut}(G)
), then the circuit constructed from
\mu
has polynomial size. Here is the result of this section:

Theorem 32
.

Fix a family
(G_{n})_{n\in\bbN}
of base graphs.
For every
n\in\bbN
, let
{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}
be a CFI-graph over
G_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n})
and let
\mu_{n}\in\text{HF}(\widehat{E}_{n})
be a h.f. set that is
CPT
-definable on input
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
(by the same
CPT
-program for the whole family of graphs). Then for every
n\in\bbN
there exists an XOR-circuit
\widehat{C}(\mu_{n})=(V_{C},E_{C})
over the edges of
G_{n}
which satisfies:

1.

The orbit-size
|\mathbf{Orb}_{G}(\widehat{C}(\mu_{n}))|
of the circuit is polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}|
.

2.

\widehat{C}(\mu_{n})
is
sensitive
to at least
\frac{|\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)|}{\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu))}
many edges in
E
.

3.

The
fan-in dimension
of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
is
{\cal O}(\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu)))
.

4.

If for every
x,y\in\text{tc}(\mu)
such that
[y]\cap x\neq\emptyset
, the space
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
has a
symmetric basis
(see Definition
56
), then the size
|V_{C}|
is polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|
.

This theorem differs from Theorem
21
for CFI-symmetric objects in two aspects. Firstly, the circuit
\widehat{C}(\mu)
is not necessarily sensitive to all edges in
\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)
but only to a logarithmic fraction of them. Secondly, we have no guarantees for the size of the circuit unless all spaces
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
admit a symmetric basis; we will introduce this concept formally in Section
6.3
. It should be noted that this fourth property mentioned in the theorem is – as far as we know – not an “if and only if”. It may be that
\widehat{C}(\mu)
has polynomial size even when the symmetric basis condition is not satisfied for
\mu
.

As a consequence, we have the following version of Theorem
31
for non-CFI-symmetric CPT-programs that decide the CFI-query. The condition that the h.f. set with large support which is used to decide the CFI-query is CFI-symmetric is weakened to the symmetric-basis condition.
As we show later, in Lemma
59
, every CFI-symmetric set also has a symmetric basis, and there are as well simple examples of non-CFI-symmetric sets with a symmetric basis (see Example
60
). Thus, the symmetric basis condition is indeed a strict generalisation of CFI-symmetry.

Theorem 33
.

Let
(G_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n}))_{n\in\bbN}
be a sequence of base graphs. Let
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
be a CFI-graph over
G_{n}
, let
\textbf{tw}_{n}
denote the treewidth of
G_{n}
, and let
f(n)\leq\textbf{tw}_{n}
be a function such that
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}
is
{\cal C}^{\textbf{tw}_{n}}
-homogeneous for all tuples of length
\leq 2f(n)
.

Let
g(n)
be a function such that the
CFI-support-gap
for every
\mu\in\text{HF}(\widehat{E}_{n})
with minimum support
s(\mu)\in\Omega(f(n))
is bounded by
g(n)
.

Let
\Pi
be a
CPT
-program that decides the CFI-query on all
{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}
using a h.f. set
\mu_{n}\in{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}
with sufficient support such that
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
has a
symmetric basis
according to Definition
56
, for all
x,y\in\text{tc}(\mu_{n})
with
[y]\cap x\neq\emptyset
.

Assume additionally that
\Pi
is
super-symmetric
or that the base graph
G_{n}
decomposes into at most
{\cal O}(\sqrt{\log|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|})
many components when any
f(n)/g(n)
many edges are removed.

Then for every
n\in\bbN
there exists an XOR-circuit
C_{n}
over
G_{n}
that satisfies the following “instantiated properties” from Theorem
32
:

1.

The number of gates in
C_{n}
is polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}|
.

2.

The orbit-size
|\mathbf{Orb}_{G_{n}}(C_{n})|
of the circuit is polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}_{n}|
.

3.

C_{n}
is
sensitive
to
\Omega(f(n)/(g(n)\cdot\log|{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}|))
many edges in
E_{n}
.

4.

The
fan-in dimension
of
C_{n}
is
{\cal O}(\log|{\mathfrak{G}}_{n}^{S}|)
.

We omit the proof of this theorem because it follows from Theorem
32
in the same way as Theorem
31
follows from Theorem
21
.

Now let us start with the proof of Theorem
32
, which spans the rest of the section. It should be noted that Theorem
21
is actually a special case of this, so we could have omitted the circuit construction for CFI-symmetric objects; however, the more general construction that we present now is not as natural as the one for CFI-symmetric objects and much harder to describe.

Fix again a base graph
G=(V,E)
, a CFI-graph
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}
over it, and an object
\mu\in\text{HF}(\widehat{E})
. This time,
\mu
need not be CFI-symmetric. In the previous subsection, we wrote
C(\mu)
for the circuit obtained by factoring out the
\sim_{E}
classes in
\text{tc}(\mu)
. Now, we denote the constructed circuit by
\widehat{C}(\mu)
. Before we explain the construction, let us look at why
C(\mu)
is not “the circuit we want” if
\mu
is not CFI-symmetric. The only place where CFI-symmetry was required in the previous subsection is in the proof of Lemma
26
, which relates the kernels of the gate matrices with the vector spaces
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x)
. This relationship is crucial because it leads to the connection between
\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)
and the sensitivity of
C(\mu)
to its input bits, and is also necessary to get a bound on the fan-in dimension of
C(\mu)
. Without such a bound, the construction would not be interesting because without fan-in restrictions, there always exist small symmetric XOR-circuits. Hence, we would like to ensure that the statement of Lemma
26
still holds for
\widehat{C}(\mu)
, even if
\mu
is not CFI-symmetric. Now take a look at the inductive proof of Lemma
26
again. The key in this induction is that for any gate matrix
M[x]
and any child
[y]
of
[x]
, the row
M[x]_{[y]-}
can be written as the product of another matrix and the child-gate-matrix
M[y]
:
M[x]_{[y]-}=(1\ 1\ ...\ 1)\cdot M[y]
. This equation holds because of the CFI-symmetry of
\mu
. Now in the general case, a similar equation will hold, namely:
M[x]_{[y]-}=N\cdot M[y]
, for some matrix
N
that has to be chosen depending on
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. So our plan for this section is as follows: We will first of all define these
N
-matrices, that essentially “repair” the proof of Lemma
26
in the non-CFI-symmetric case. Based on these matrices and on
C(\mu)
, we will construct
\widehat{C}(\mu)
by introducing gadgets that simulate the effect of the chosen
N
-matrices. Then this circuit will be exactly such that the proof of Lemma
26
goes through again, even if
\mu
is not CFI-symmetric. In a sense, we can view the gadgets as corrections for local violations of CFI-symmetry.

6.1
Definition of the matrices

Actually, we will not only define the said
N
-matrices, but also, for every
[x]\in\{[x]\mid x\in\text{tc}(\mu)\}
, a Boolean matrix
M[x]
. We keep the notation
M[x]
from the previous subsection, even though, strictly speaking,
M[x]
will not be the gate matrix of any gate in
\widehat{C}(\mu)
; rather, it will be a matrix that satisfies
\textbf{Ker}(M[x])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x)
, and it will serve as a kind of construction specification for a gadget in
\widehat{C}(\mu)
. The
M[x]
-matrices that we are going to define depend on the
M
- and
N
-matrices of the connected components of
x
in the object
\mu
. Therefore, the construction of these matrices proceeds inductively from the atoms of
\mu
to the more deeply nested sets. At this point, our main objective is to build the matrices in such a way that their kernels correspond to the stabiliser spaces of the sets they belong to. Furthermore, the matrices should satisfy certain symmetry requirements with respect to the action of
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\leq\mathbf{Aut}(G)
. Only after the construction of the matrices, we will use them to construct the circuit
\widehat{C}(\mu)
in such a way that the gate matrices of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
are as desired. So the procedure in this subsection is the other way round as in the previous one, where the circuit came first, and we then analysed its gate matrices. Now we are specifying the matrices first, and then build the circuit so that the statement of Lemma
26
holds for
\widehat{C}(\mu)
by construction.
Along with the matrices, we will provide certain group homomorphisms that ensure symmetry.
To speak about symmetry of matrices, we introduce notation to express the effect of row- and column-permutations:

Let
M\in\bbF_{2}^{I\times J}
be any Boolean matrix, and
\sigma:I\longrightarrow I^{\prime},\pi:J\longrightarrow J^{\prime}
be bijections. Then
(\sigma,\pi)(M)\in\bbF_{2}^{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is the matrix with
(\sigma,\pi)(M)_{\sigma i,\pi j}=M_{i,j}
for each
(i,j)\in I\times J
. In particular, if
\sigma\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I),\pi\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(J)
, then
(\sigma,\pi)(M)
is the matrix that arises from
M
when the respective row- and column-permutations are applied.

Proposition 34
.

Let
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\bbF_{2}^{I}
be two Boolean vectors and
\pi\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I)
a permutation of its entries. Then

\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{w}=\pi(\mathbf{v}^{T})\cdot\pi(\mathbf{w}).

Therefore, if
M\in\bbF_{2}^{I\times J}
is a matrix and
\mathbf{w}\in\bbF_{2}^{J}
is a vector, then

\sigma(M\cdot\mathbf{w})=(\sigma,\pi)M\cdot\pi(\mathbf{w}),

for every
\sigma\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I),\pi\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(J)
.

This proposition follows immediately from the definition of the scalar product of vectors because
\mathbf{v}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{w}
and
\pi(\mathbf{v}^{T})\cdot\pi(\mathbf{w})
are the same sum of products, just summed in a different order. The statement about matrix-vector-multiplication then follows because this is just the scalar product of every row vector with
\mathbf{w}
. The proposition will sometimes be used without explicit reference in this section.

In the rest of this section, we will often speak about the following orbits and stabilisers. They differ from the ones from the previous section in so far as they concern the subgroup
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\leq\mathbf{Aut}(G)
, instead of
\mathbf{Aut}(G)
itself. Thus, we override the notation from the previous section. Let
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{Orb}_{G}([x])

\displaystyle:=\{\pi([x])\mid\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\leq\mathbf{Aut}(G)\}.

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([x])

\displaystyle:=\{\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\mid\pi([x])=[x]\}.

Similarly, for
x,y\in\text{tc}(\mu)
,
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
refers to the stabiliser of the set
[y]\cap x
in the group
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
.

Any orbit of a
\sim
-class is a set of
\sim
-equivalence classes:

Lemma 35
.

For any
\sim_{E}
-class
[x]\subseteq\text{tc}(\mu)
, and any
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
,
\pi([x])
is also a
\sim_{E}
-class of
\text{tc}(\mu)
.

Proof.

It is not hard to check that for any
\pi\in\mathbf{Aut}(G)
, and any
\rho_{F}\in\mathbf{Aut}_{\text{CFI}}({\mathfrak{G}})
it holds
\rho_{\pi F}=\pi\circ\rho_{F}\circ\pi^{-1}
.
Let
x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in[x]
and let
\rho_{F}\in\mathbf{Aut}_{\text{CFI}}({\mathfrak{G}})
be such that
\rho_{F}(x^{\prime})=x^{\prime\prime}
. Then
\rho_{\pi F}(\pi x^{\prime})=\pi x^{\prime\prime}
by the above equation. Thus,
\pi(x^{\prime})\sim_{E}\pi(x^{\prime\prime})
. Similarly one can show that if
x^{\prime}\not\sim_{E}x^{\prime\prime}
, then
\pi(x^{\prime})\not\sim_{E}\pi(x^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore,
\pi([x])
is again a
\sim_{E}
-class. The fact that
\pi([x])\subseteq\text{tc}(\mu)
follows because
\pi
extends to an automorphism of the h.f. set
\mu
.
∎

Corollary 36
.

Let
x,y\in\text{tc}(\mu)
such that
[y]\cap x\neq\emptyset
. Then

\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]).

Proof.

The group
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
maps the set
[y]\cap x\subseteq[y]
to itself, so it does not move this subset of the
\sim
-class
[y]
into another
\sim
-class. Then by Lemma
35
, it must map the whole class
[y]
to itself because the image of
[y]
must again be a
\sim
-class.
∎

Lemma 37
.

For any
x,x^{\prime},y\in\text{tc}(\mu)
such that
x\sim_{E}x^{\prime}
and
[y]\cap x\neq\emptyset
, it holds

\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x^{\prime}).

Proof.

The sets
[y]\cap x
and
[y]\cap x^{\prime}
are related via an automorphism in
\mathbf{Aut}_{\text{CFI}}({\mathfrak{G}})
. Therefore,
Lemma
7
applied to the set
[y]\cap x
yields the desired statement.
∎

Now we come to the inductive definition of the aforementioned
M
- and
N
-matrices. Here is the precise list of objects that we are going to define:

(a)

For every
[x]\in\{[x]\mid x\in\text{tc}(\mu)\}
:

(i)

An index-set
I_{[x]}
.

(ii)

A matrix
M[x]\in\bbF_{2}^{I_{[x]}\times E}
with the property that
\textbf{Ker}(M[x])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x)
(note that
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x)=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x^{\prime})
for every
x^{\prime}\in[x]
by Lemma
7
).

Let
{\cal C}[x]:=\{[y]\mid[y]\cap x\neq\emptyset\}
(note that this does not depend on the representative of
[x]
– see Lemma
22
) denote the
\sim
-classes of the connected components of
x
.

(b)

For every
[x]\in\{[x]\mid x\in\text{tc}(\mu)\}
, where
x
is a set,
and every
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
:

(i)

An index-set
J_{[x][y]}
.

(ii)

A matrix
N[x][y]\in\bbF_{2}^{J_{[x][y]}\times I_{[y]}}
with the property that
\textbf{Ker}(N[x][y]\cdot M[y])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
(by Lemma
37
,
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
is independent of the choice of representative of
[x]
).

(c)

For every orbit
\Omega_{[x]}:=\mathbf{Orb}_{G}([x])
, let
I_{\Omega_{[x]}}:=\biguplus_{[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}}I_{[x^{\prime}]}
. For every
\Omega_{[x]}
, we provide a group homomorphism
g_{[x]}:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{\Omega_{[x]}})
such that for each
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
and each
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
, it holds
g_{[x]}(\pi)(I_{[x^{\prime}]})=I_{\pi[x^{\prime}]}
.
M\pi[x^{\prime}]=(g_{[x]},\pi)M[x^{\prime}]
for each
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
and
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
.

(d)

For every orbit
\Omega_{[x]}
, let

J_{\Omega_{[x]}}:=\biguplus_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}}}{{[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]}}}J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}.

For every
\Omega_{[x]}
, we provide a group homomorphism
h_{[x]}:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(J_{\Omega_{[x]}})
such that for each
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
and each
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
, it holds
h_{[x]}(\pi)(J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]})=J_{\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]}
. Furthermore,
N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]=(h_{[x]}(\pi),g_{[x]}(\pi))N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
for each
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
,
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
, and
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
.

The role of the group homomorphisms is to ensure – when we build the circuit from these matrices – that every
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
that acts on the input gates indeed extends to an automorphism of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
. Before we actually construct anything, we have to verify that it is indeed possible to satisfy the symmetry conditions witnessed by the group homomorphisms and the conditions on the kernels of the matrices simultaneously. In other words, we have to show that the stabiliser spaces, which are supposed to be equal to the respective kernels, are mapped to each other by the permutations in
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
:

Lemma 38
.

Let
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
and
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
. Then

\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(\pi x)=\pi(\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x))=\{\pi\mathbf{v}\mid\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x)\},

where
\pi\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(E)
acts on vectors in
\bbF_{2}^{E}
by permuting their entries. Furthermore, for every
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
, we have

\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(\pi[y]\cap\pi x)=\pi(\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)).

Proof.

For any
\pi\in\mathbf{Aut}(G)
, and any
\rho_{F}\in\mathbf{Aut}_{\text{CFI}}({\mathfrak{G}})
, it holds
\rho_{\pi F}=\pi\circ\rho_{F}\circ\pi^{-1}
. Thus,
\rho_{\pi F}\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(\pi x)
if and only if
(\pi\circ\rho_{F}\circ\pi^{-1})(\pi x)=\pi x
. This holds if and only if
(\pi\circ\rho_{F})(x)=\pi x
, which is the case iff
\rho_{F}(x)=x
. This proves the first part of the lemma since
\chi(\pi F)=\pi(\chi(F))
. The second part can be shown in the same way because
\pi[y]\cap\pi x=\pi([y]\cap x)
. This last equation holds since the action of
\pi
on
\text{HF}(\widehat{E})
is a bijection from
\text{HF}(\widehat{E})
to itself, so
\pi[y]\cap\pi x\subseteq\pi([y]\cap x)
(this would not necessarily be true if
\pi
were not injective on
\text{HF}(\widehat{E})
).
∎

Inductive construction

Base case:

Let
x=e_{i}
, for
e\in E
and
i\in\{0,1\}
, be an atom in
\text{tc}(\mu)
. Then we set

M[x]:=\chi(e)^{T}.

Formally, we define the row index set as
I_{[x]}:=\{[x]\}
, but any singleton set that is distinct from all other index sets will do.

Now for every orbit
\Omega_{[x]}
, where
x
is an atom in
\text{tc}(\mu)
, we define the homomorphism
g_{[x]}:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{\Omega_{[x]}})
by letting
g_{[x]}(\pi)([x^{\prime}]):=[\pi x^{\prime}]
for every
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
,
[x^{\prime}]\in I_{\Omega_{[x]}}
(note that by definition of the index-sets
I_{[x]}
,
I_{\Omega_{[x]}}
is equal to the orbit
\Omega_{[x]}
).

Inductive step:

We deal with the items from the above list in the order (b), (d), (a), (c).
Let
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
be a non-atomic object, that is, a set. Assume that for every
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
and every
[y]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
, for every
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
, the respective matrix
M[y]
with index set
I_{[y]}
has been constructed. Thus we also assume that for any such
[y]
, the homomorphism
g_{[y]}
corresponding to
\Omega_{[y]}
has been defined. We fix a
y
such that
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
. For this fixed pair
[x],[y]
we will now construct the matrix
N[x][y]
. Then we will close it under the action of
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
. That is, given this matrix
N[x][y]
, we will symmetrically define
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
for all
[y^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[y]}
, and all
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
such that
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
.

After this, there may still exist some components
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x]
for which
N[x][y^{\prime}]
has not been defined. In that case, we fix such a
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x]
, define the corresponding matrix
N[x][y^{\prime}]
explicitly, and define the matrices for all
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
-images of
[x]
and
[y^{\prime}]
symmetrically, and so on. Hence, we first have to describe how to define the respective initial matrix from which we obtain the other ones by symmetry.

Definition of the N-matrices

Let
y\in\text{tc}(\mu)
be such that
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
. We assume that
M[y]
,
I_{[y]}
and
g_{[y]}
have been constructed. The matrix
N[x][y]
is defined as the smallest Boolean matrix that satisfies the following two conditions:

(i)

\textbf{Ker}(N[x][y])\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)=\{(M[y]\cdot\mathbf{v})\mid\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)\}.

(For a matrix
M\in\bbF_{2}^{I\times J}
,
\textbf{Im}(M)
denotes the space that is the image of
\bbF_{2}^{J}
under
M
).

(ii)

There exists a homomorphism
h
from
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))\leq{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
into the symmetric group on the row index set of
N[x][y]
such that for every
\sigma\in g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
, it holds
(h(\sigma),\sigma)(N[x][y])=N[x][y]
.

In the second property, we abused notation and wrote
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
for a subgroup of
{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
, even though
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
is formally a subgroup of
{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{\Omega_{[y]}})
. However, we know from Corollary
36
that
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y])
, so
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
indeed maps the row index set of
M[y]
, that is,
I_{[y]}
, to itself (see property (c) of
g_{[y]}
that holds by the induction hypothesis).

By “smallest” matrix we mean one that satisfies (i) and (ii) and has the least number of rows. If there are multiple such matrices with the same minimal number of rows, we choose an arbitrary one of them for
N[x][y]
.

Let
m
be the number of rows of
N[x][y]
. We define the row index set
J_{[x][y]}
of
N[x][y]
as an
m
-element set that is disjoint from all other index sets constructed so far. Formally, this can be achieved by letting
J_{[x][y]}:=\{(i,[x],[y])\mid i\in[m]\}
.

We have to show that there always exists a matrix that satisfies (a) and (b). A matrix satisfying (a) can be found with methods from linear algebra:

Lemma 39
.

Let
\Gamma\leq\Delta\leq\bbF_{2}^{I}
be Boolean vector spaces. Let
d
be the dimension of
\Gamma
and
k=\dim\Delta-d
be the codimension of
\Gamma
in
\Delta
. There exists a matrix
N\in\bbF_{2}^{[k]\times I}
such that
\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\Delta=\Gamma
.

Proof.

Each of the
k
rows of
N
can be obtained as the solution to a linear equation system. For
i\in[k]
,
j\in I
, let
n_{ij}:=N_{ij}
denote the sought entry in row
i
and column
j
. Fix a basis
{\cal B}_{\Gamma}
of
\Gamma
, and an extension of that basis
{\cal B}\supseteq{\cal B}_{\Gamma}
such that
{\cal B}={\cal B}_{\Gamma}\uplus\{\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{k}\}
is a basis of
\Delta
. For each
i\in[k]
, we define an equation system
A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
whose unique solution vector is the desired row
(n_{i1},n_{i2},...,n_{i|I|})
of
N
. The system has
\dim\Delta
many equations, where each equation is associated with a basis vector in
{\cal B}
. For every basis vector
\mathbf{v}\in{\cal B}\setminus\{\mathbf{w}_{i}\}
, we have the equation

\sum_{j\in I}\mathbf{v}(j)\cdot\mathbf{x}(j)=0

in the system
A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
. For the basis vector
\mathbf{w}_{i}\in{\cal B}
, we have the equation

\sum_{j\in I}\mathbf{w}_{i}(j)\cdot\mathbf{x}(j)=1

in
A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
. In this way, we define
k
equation systems, one for each
i\in[k]
. In fact, the coefficient matrix
A_{i}
is the same for all of them. Its rows are the vectors in
{\cal B}
(transposed). The vector
\mathbf{b}_{i}
has a
1
-entry in the row containing
\mathbf{w}_{i}
, and is zero otherwise. The rank and the number of rows of every
A_{i}
is
\dim\Delta
because
{\cal B}
is a basis of
\Delta
. Hence, each of the equation systems has a unique solution. If we define each entry
n_{ij}
of
N
to be the
j
-th entry of the solution vector to
A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
, then indeed,
\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\Delta=\Gamma
, by definition of the equation systems.
∎

This shows that a matrix satisfying condition (a) always exists. The matrix can be closed under the action of
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
so that it also satisfies condition (b). This requires that the vector space that we want as the kernel of
N[x][y]
is invariant under that permutation group:

Lemma 40
.

The space
M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)\leq\bbF_{2}^{I_{[y]}}
is invariant under the action of the permutation group
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))\leq{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
on the entries of its vectors. That is, for every
\mathbf{v}\in M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
and
\pi\in g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
, it holds
\pi(\mathbf{v})\in M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{v}\in M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
and
\pi\in g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
. We can write
\mathbf{v}=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{w}
for some
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. Fix a
\sigma\in g^{-1}_{[y]}(\pi)
, i.e.
\sigma\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
. By Proposition
34
it holds:

(\pi,\sigma)(M[y])\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{w})=\pi(\mathbf{v}).

From the inductive hypothesis we have that
(\pi,\sigma)(M[y])=M[y]
since
\sigma\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y])
(see item (c) in the enumeration above). The fact that
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y])
is shown in Corollary
36
. We conclude:
M[y]\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{w})=\pi(\mathbf{v})
. If
\sigma(\mathbf{w})\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
, then we are done and have that
\pi(\mathbf{v})\in M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
, as desired. To show that
\sigma(\mathbf{w})\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
, we apply Lemma
38
: Since
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
, we have
\sigma\mathbf{w}\in\sigma(\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x))=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(\sigma[y]\cap\sigma x)
. Finally, as mentioned in the proof of Lemma
38
, we have
\sigma[y]\cap\sigma x=\sigma([y]\cap x)
, and it holds
\sigma([y]\cap x)=[y]\cap x
, because
\sigma\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
.
∎

Knowing this, we can see that it is indeed possible to satisfy both conditions (i) and (ii) at the same time.

Lemma 41
.

Let
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
. There exists a Boolean matrix
N
that satisfies conditions (i) and (ii) mentioned above, i.e.:

(i)

\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x).

(ii)

There exists a homomorphism
h
from
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))\leq{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
into the symmetric group on the row index set of
N
such that for every
\sigma\in g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
, it holds
(h(\sigma),\sigma)(N)=N
.

Proof.

Lemma
39
applied to
\Delta=\textbf{Im}(M[y])
and
\Gamma=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
gives us a matrix
N^{\prime}\in\bbF_{2}^{[k]\times I_{[y]}}
that satisfies condition (i); here,
k=\dim(\textbf{Im}\ M[y])-\dim(M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x))
. We can close
N^{\prime}
under the action of
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))\leq{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
so that condition (ii) is also satisfied: For each row
N^{\prime}_{i-}
of
N^{\prime}
, let
\mathbf{Orb}(N^{\prime}_{i-}):=\{\pi(N^{\prime}_{i-})\mid\pi\in g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))\}
. Here,
\pi\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
permutes the columns, i.e. the entries of the respective row
N^{\prime}_{i-}
. Now let
N
be the Boolean matrix whose set of rows is the disjoint union
\biguplus_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{Orb}(N^{\prime}_{i-})
. Clearly, there is a homomorphism
h
from
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
into the symmetric group on the rows of
N
(more precisely into
\Pi_{i\in[k]}{\mathbf{Sym}}(\mathbf{Orb}(N^{\prime}_{i-}))
). This homomorphism is just the group action of
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
on the rows of
N
(separately on the orbits
\mathbf{Orb}(N^{\prime}_{i-})
).

It remains to show that this symmetry-closed matrix
N
still satisfies condition (i), i.e. that
\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. We have
\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])\subseteq M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
because for any vector
\mathbf{v}\notin M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
, either
\mathbf{v}\notin\textbf{Im}(M[y])
, or if
\mathbf{v}\in\textbf{Im}(M[y])
, then
N^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{0}
because
\textbf{Ker}(N^{\prime})\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. Since
N^{\prime}
is a submatrix of
N
, we also have
N\cdot\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{0}
, so
\mathbf{v}\notin\textbf{Ker}(N)
. Therefore,
\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])\subseteq M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. It remains to show:
M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)\subseteq\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])
. So let
\mathbf{v}\in M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. Then
N^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}
. We have to prove that for every row
N^{\prime}_{i-}
and every
\pi\in g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
, we have
\pi(N^{\prime}_{i-})\cdot\mathbf{v}=0
. It holds (see Proposition
34
):

\pi(N^{\prime}_{i-})\cdot\mathbf{v}=\pi^{-1}(\pi(N^{\prime}_{i-}))\cdot\pi^{-1}\mathbf{v}=N^{\prime}_{i-}\cdot\pi^{-1}\mathbf{v}=0.

The final equality holds because
\textbf{Ker}(N^{\prime})=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
, and
\pi^{-1}\mathbf{v}\in M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
by Lemma
40
. Since each row of
N
is of the form
\pi(N^{\prime}_{i-})
for some row
i
of
N^{\prime}
and
\pi\in g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
, we have shown that
M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)\subseteq\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])
.
∎

This lemma shows that there exists a Boolean matrix satisfying conditions (i) and (ii), so it is indeed possible to pick a smallest one for
N[x][y]
. The trouble is that we do not know a priori how small it is. Therefore, the construction of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
that we are describing does not come with a guaranteed size bound. Later on in Section
6.3
we will get back to the choice of
N[x][y]
and show how we can bound its size in case that
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
has a symmetric basis. The idea will be that the size of the closure of
N^{\prime}
under the action of
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
can be bounded then, because the symmetries of the basis of
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
“propagate” through the equation systems
A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
that are used to define the rows of
N^{\prime}
.

Since at least one matrix satisfying (i) and (ii) exists,
N[x][y]
can indeed be defined as the smallest one. Remember that this definition was for a fixed
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
. Now let
[y^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[y]}
, and
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
such that
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
and such that there is a
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
with
\pi([y]\cap x)=[y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime}
(it may be that
[x^{\prime}]=[x]
). Set
\pi_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}:=\pi
, so we can refer back to this particular permutation in the future.
We set
J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}:=\{(i,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])\mid i\in[m]\}
. Here,
m
still denotes the number of rows of the previously defined
N[x][y]
.
Let
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
be the Boolean matrix in
\bbF_{2}^{J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}\times I_{[y^{\prime}]}}
such that
(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{(k,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]),g_{[y]}(\pi_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]})(i)}=(N[x][y])_{(k,[x],[y]),i}
for every
i\in I_{[y]}
and
k\in[m]
. We will usually write
N([x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{k,-}
instead of
(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{(k,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]),-}
to denote the
k
-th row of the matrix.
In this way, we define the matrices
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
for all
[y^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[y]}
. We will call the
\sim
-class
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
, that we arbitrarily chose as the first one in its orbit to define
N[x][y]
with Lemma
41
, the
primer
of the orbit
\Omega_{[y]}
. We proceed to pick a new primer
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
for which
N[x][y]
has not been defined so far, and repeat the construction for
[y]
and its orbit
\Omega_{[y]}
. This is done until
N[x][y]
is defined for every
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
and for every
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
, for every
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
.

We show that the defined matrices have the desired properties:

Lemma 42
.

Let
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
and
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
. Then

\textbf{Ker}(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]\cdot M[y^{\prime}])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime}).

Proof.

Let
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
be the primer of the orbit
\Omega_{[y^{\prime}]}
that was used in the matrix construction. Then
[y^{\prime}]=\pi_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}[y]
and
[x^{\prime}]=\pi_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}[x]
. If
[y^{\prime}]=[y]
, then
\pi_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}
is the identity permutation in
{\mathbf{Sym}}(V)
. For ease of notation, we write
\pi:=\pi_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}
in the following. By Lemma
38
, we have

\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime})=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(\pi[y]\cap\pi x)=\pi(\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)).

By definition of
N[x][y]
, and because
\textbf{Ker}(M[y])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y)\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
, we have
\textbf{Ker}(N[x][y]\cdot M[y])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. It holds
M[y^{\prime}]=(g_{[y]}(\pi),\pi)M[y]
(item (c) of the inductive hypothesis). So for any vector
\mathbf{v}\in\bbF_{2}^{E}
, we have
M[y^{\prime}]\cdot\pi(\mathbf{v})=g_{[y]}(\pi)(M[y]\cdot\mathbf{v})
. Here,
g_{[y]}(\pi)
acts on a vector
\mathbf{w}\in\bbF_{2}^{I_{[y]}}
by mapping it to a vector
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\bbF_{2}^{I_{[y^{\prime}]}}
with
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}(g_{[y]}(\pi)(i))=\mathbf{w}(i)
for every
i\in I_{[y]}
. By definition, we have for the
k
-th row of
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
:
N([x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{k,-}=g_{[y]}(\pi)(N([x][y])_{k,-})
. In total, this means that for every
\mathbf{v}\in\bbF_{2}^{E}
, it holds:

N([x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{k,-}\cdot M[y^{\prime}]\cdot\pi(\mathbf{v})=N([x][y])_{k,-}\cdot M[y]\cdot\mathbf{v}.

Therefore,
\textbf{Ker}(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]\cdot M[y^{\prime}])=\pi(\textbf{Ker}(N[x][y]\cdot M[y]))=\pi(\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x))=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime})
.
∎

The proofs of the next lemmas are given in the appendix. They concern the symmetries of the constructed
N
-matrices, and essentially follow directly from the construction of the first
N
-matrix and the fact that the other
N
-matrices are symmetric to it. The formal proofs involve tedious calculations, though.

Lemma 43
.

Let
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
and
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
. There exists a homomorphism
h^{\prime}
from
g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime}))\leq{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y^{\prime}]})
into the symmetric group on the row index set of
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
such that for every
\sigma\in g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime}))
, it holds
(h^{\prime}(\sigma),\sigma)(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])=N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
.

Proof sketch.

Let
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
be the primer of
\Omega_{[y^{\prime}]}
. Then for
N[x][y]
, the lemma holds by construction because we explicitly closed the rows of
N[x][y]
under these symmetries. For
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
, the result follows by symmetry of the construction.
∎

Lemmas
42
and
43
assert that all the constructed matrices
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
satisfy the properties (i) and (ii) that
N[x][y]
has by construction.

Now let

J_{\Omega_{[x]}}:=\bigcup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}}}{{[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]}}}J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}.

We provide a group homomorphism
h_{[x]}:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(J_{\Omega_{[x]}})
such that for each
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
and each
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
, it holds
h_{[x]}(\pi)(J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]})=J_{\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]}
. Furthermore, we want that
(N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}])=(h_{[x]}(\pi),g_{[x]}(\pi))N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
for each
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
,
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
, and
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
.
For each triple
(k,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])\in J_{\Omega_{[x]}}
, we set:

h_{[x]}(\pi)(k,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]):=(\ell,\pi[x^{\prime}],\pi[y^{\prime}]),

where
\ell
is defined as follows: Let

t:=|\{i\in\{1,2,...,k-1\}\mid(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{i,-}=(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{k,-}\}|.

That is, for the
k
-th row of
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
, there are
t
rows identical to it with a smaller index. Then
\ell
is defined such that

|\{i\in\{1,2,...,\ell-1\}\mid(N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}])_{i,-}=g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\pi)((N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{k,-})\}|=t,

and such that
(N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}])_{\ell,-}=g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\pi)((N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{k,-})
. In other words,
\ell
is the
(t+1)
st row of
N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]
which is equal to the
k
-th row of
N[x][y]
, up to a permutation of the columns given by
g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\pi)
.

We have to argue that
h_{[x]}(\pi)(k,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])
is indeed well-defined:

Lemma 44
.

Let
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]},[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
. Let
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
. Then for every
(k,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])\in J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}
, the number of rows of
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
which are equal to the
k
-th row is the same as the number of rows of
N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]
that are equal to the
k
-th row of
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
, up to application of
g_{[y^{\prime}]}
to the columns. Formally:

\displaystyle|\{(i,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])\in J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}\mid(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{(i,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]),-}=(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{k,-}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\{(i,\pi[x^{\prime}],\pi[y^{\prime}])\in J_{\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]}\mid(N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}])_{(i,\pi[x^{\prime}],\pi[y^{\prime}]),-}=g_{[y^{\prime}]}((N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{k,-})\}|

Proof sketch.
Follows again from the fact that
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
and
N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]
are by construction symmetric to each other. ∎

This lemma shows that we can indeed define
h_{[x]}
as we did. Now one can verify that
h_{[x]}
is a group homomorphism with the desired properties. Again, we prove this in the appendix; it follows from the definition of
h_{[x]}
and Lemma
44
.

Lemma 45
.

The mapping
h_{[x]}:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(J_{\Omega_{[x]}})
is a group homomorphism.
For every
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
,
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
and each
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
, it holds
h_{[x]}(\pi)(J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]})=J_{\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]}
. Furthermore,
N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]=(h_{[x]}(\pi),g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\pi))N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
for each
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
,
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
, and
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
.

This finishes the construction and correctness proof of the matrices
N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]
for all
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
and
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
and of the associated homomorphism
h_{[x]}:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\longrightarrow J_{\Omega_{[x]}}
. Items (b) and (d) of the inductive step are thus covered. To complete the inductive step we still have to define the matrices
M[x^{\prime}]
for all
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
.

Definition of the M-matrices

Let
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
. Informally,
M[x^{\prime}]
is obtained by collecting all the rows of the matrices
(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]\cdot M[y^{\prime}])
, for all
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
, and putting them together as the rows of
M[x^{\prime}]
. Formally, let

I_{[x^{\prime}]}:=\biguplus_{[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]}J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}.

Note that by construction, the sets
J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]},J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime\prime}]}
are pairwise disjoint if
[y^{\prime}]\neq[y^{\prime\prime}]
. Then the rows of
M[x^{\prime}]\in\bbF_{2}^{I_{[x^{\prime}]}\times E}
are defined as follows: For
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
and
(i,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])\in J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}
, we let

M[x^{\prime}]_{(i,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]),-}:=(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]\cdot M[y^{\prime}])_{(i,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]),-}.

This matrix has the desired kernel:

Lemma 46
.

For every
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
, the matrix
M[x^{\prime}]
defined as above satisfies:

\textbf{Ker}(M[x^{\prime}])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x^{\prime}).

Proof.

By definition of
M[x^{\prime}]
, a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\bbF_{2}^{E}
is in
\textbf{Ker}(M[x^{\prime}])
if and only if

\mathbf{v}\in\textbf{Ker}(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]\cdot M[y^{\prime}])
for all
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
. By Lemma
42
, this is the case iff
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime})
, for all
[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]
. That is to say,

\mathbf{v}\in\bigcap_{[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]}\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime}).

This is equivalent to
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(x^{\prime})
because
x^{\prime}=\bigcup_{[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]}([y^{\prime}]\cap x^{\prime})
(see Proposition
25
).
∎

Finally, we have to provide the homomorphism
g_{[x]}:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{\Omega_{[x]}})
, where
I_{\Omega_{[x]}}=\biguplus_{[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}}I_{[x^{\prime}]}
.
Note that
I_{\Omega_{[x]}}=J_{\Omega_{[x]}}
by definition of the index sets
I_{[x^{\prime}]}
.
Therefore we can simply set
g_{[x]}:=h_{[x]}
. This homomorphism indeed satisfies the desired properties:

Lemma 47
.

For each
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
and each
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
, it holds
g_{[x]}(\pi)(I_{[x^{\prime}]})=I_{\pi[x^{\prime}]}
. Furthermore,
M\pi[x^{\prime}]=(g_{[x]}(\pi),\pi)M[x^{\prime}]
.

Proof.

Let
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
and
[x^{\prime}]\in\Omega_{[x]}
. Due to Lemma
35
,
\pi[x^{\prime}]
is again a
\sim
-class in
\text{tc}(\mu)
. Because
\pi
extends to an automorphism of
\mu
, it also holds

\pi({\cal C}[x^{\prime}])=\{\pi[y^{\prime}]\mid[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]\}={\cal C}(\pi[x^{\prime}]).

Hence, by Lemma
45
,
h_{[x]}(\pi)
maps the set
I_{[x^{\prime}]}=\biguplus_{[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}[x^{\prime}]}J_{[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]}
to the set
I_{\pi[x^{\prime}]}=\biguplus_{\pi[y^{\prime}]\in{\cal C}\pi[x^{\prime}]}J_{\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]}
. It remains to show
(M\pi[x^{\prime}])_{g_{[x]}(\pi)(i),\pi(e)}=M[x^{\prime}]_{i,e}
for each
i=(j,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])\in I_{[x^{\prime}]}
, and
e\in E
.
By definition of the
M
-matrices, we have:

\displaystyle(M\pi[x^{\prime}])_{g_{[x]}(\pi)(i),\pi(e)}=(M\pi[x^{\prime}])_{(j^{\prime},\pi[x^{\prime}],\pi[y^{\prime}]),\pi(e)}

\displaystyle=(N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]\cdot M\pi[y^{\prime}])_{(j^{\prime},\pi[x^{\prime}],\pi[y^{\prime}]),\pi(e)}

\displaystyle=(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}]\cdot M[y^{\prime}])_{(j,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]),e}

\displaystyle=M[x^{\prime}]_{i,e}.

Here,
j^{\prime}
is such that
(j^{\prime},\pi[x^{\prime}],\pi[y^{\prime}])=g_{[x]}(\pi)(j,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])=h_{[x]}(\pi)(j,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}])
. The final equality holds for the following reason:
(N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]\cdot M\pi[y^{\prime}])_{(j^{\prime},\pi[x^{\prime}],\pi[y^{\prime}]),\pi(e)}
is the product of row
j^{\prime}
of
N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}]
with column
\pi(e)
of
M\pi[y^{\prime}]
. The lemma we are currently proving already holds for
M[y^{\prime}]
by induction hypothesis, so
(M\pi[y^{\prime}])_{-\pi(e)}=g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\pi)(M[y^{\prime}]_{-e})
. Also, we have
(N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}])_{j^{\prime},-}=g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\pi)((N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{j,-})
by Lemma
45
. Then Proposition
34
tells us that
(N\pi[x^{\prime}]\pi[y^{\prime}])_{j^{\prime},-}\cdot(M\pi[y^{\prime}])_{-,\pi(e)}=(N[x^{\prime}][y^{\prime}])_{j,-}\cdot(M[y^{\prime}])_{-e}
because these vector products are really the same sums of products, where the summands are just reordered by
g_{[y^{\prime}]}(\pi)
.
∎

6.2
Construction of the circuit

We construct the circuit
\widehat{C}(\mu)=(V_{C},E_{C})
from the matrices that we have defined in the previous section.
The set of gates is

V_{C}:=\biguplus_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x\in\text{tc}(\mu)}}{{(i,[x],[y])\in I_{[x]}}}}\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}.

So every row of any of the
M[x]
-matrices with index
(i,[x],[y])
will correspond to a gate
\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}
. The construction will ensure that

\chi({\cal X}(\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}))=M[x]_{(i,[x],[y]),-}^{T}.

Thus, the gate will compute the XOR over precisely the input gates labelled with edges that have a
1
-entry in the row
M[x]_{(i,[x],[y]),-}
.

For every atom
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
, we have
I_{[x]}=\{[x]\}
. We define the corresponding gate
\mathbf{g}_{[x]}
as an input gate of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
with
\ell(\mathbf{g}_{[x]})=e
, where
e\in E
is the edge such that
[x]\subseteq\{e_{0},e_{1}\}
.

If
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
is not an atom, then for every
(i,[x],[y])\in I_{[x]}
,
\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}
is an internal gate that has incoming wires from exactly those gates
\mathbf{g}_{j,[y],[z]}
such that the matrix
N[x][y]
has a
1
-entry in row
(i,[x],[y])
and column
(j,[y],[z])
. Note that
(j,[y],[z])\in I_{[y]}
, so
[z]\in{\cal C}[y]
. Formally, the set of children of
\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}
is:

\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}E_{C}:=\{\mathbf{g}_{j,[y],[z]}\mid(j,[y],[z])\in I_{[y]},(N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),(j,[y],[z])}=1\}.

It remains to specify the root of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
. Consider the matrix
M[\mu]
. Let
(i,[\mu],[y])
be a row of
M[\mu]
with a maximum number of one-entries. We define the root
r
to be the gate
\mathbf{g}_{i,[\mu],[y]}
.

The figure below shows the matrices and XOR-gates for an object
x
consisting of two connected components
[y]\cap x
and
[y^{\prime}]\cap x
. The matrices
N[x][y]
and
N[x][y^{\prime}]
have just one row each in this example, and we are assuming that
{\cal C}[y]=\{[z]\}
and
{\cal C}[y^{\prime}]=\{[z^{\prime}]\}
, and that
M[y]=N[y][z]\cdot M[z]
has three rows and
M[y^{\prime}]=N[y^{\prime}][z^{\prime}]\cdot M[z^{\prime}]
has two rows.

x=\{\underbrace{y_{1},y_{2}},\underbrace{y^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{2}}\}
[y]
[y^{\prime}]
M[x]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}(N[x][y]\cdot M[y])\\
(N[x][y^{\prime}]\cdot M[y^{\prime}])\end{array}\right]
M[y]
M[y^{\prime}]
N[x][y]=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&1&1\end{array}\right]
N[x][y^{\prime}]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\end{array}\right]
\oplus\mathbf{g}_{1,[x],[y]}
\oplus\mathbf{g}_{1,[x],[y^{\prime}]}
\oplus\mathbf{g}_{1,[y],[z]}
\oplus\mathbf{g}_{2,[y],[z]}
\oplus\mathbf{g}_{3,[y],[z]}
\oplus\mathbf{g}_{1,[y^{\prime}],[z^{\prime}]}
\oplus\mathbf{g}_{2,[y^{\prime}],[z^{\prime}]}

Figure 2:
Example showing how the XOR-gates are connected according to the
N
-matrices.

Now we prove that
\widehat{C}(\mu)
has the desired properties.

Lemma 48
.

Every
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
extends to an automorphism of the circuit
\widehat{C}(\mu)
, that is:

\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\widehat{C}(\mu)).

Proof.

Let
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
. We claim that the following mapping
\sigma:V_{C}\longrightarrow V_{C}
is an automorphism of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
that
\pi
extends to. We let

\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}):=\mathbf{g}_{g_{[x]}(\pi)(i,[x],[y])},

where
g_{[x]}:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{\Omega_{[x]}})
is the group homomorphism for
\Omega_{[x]}
from the construction of the matrices in the previous subsection. We have to show three things about
\sigma
. Firstly, that
\sigma\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(V_{C})
. Secondly, that
\sigma
maps wires to wires and non-wires to non-wires of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
. Finally, that for every input gate
\mathbf{g}_{[x]}
it holds:
\ell(\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{[x]}))=\pi(\ell(\mathbf{g}_{[x]}))
. This last statement actually follows directly from the definition of
g_{[x]}
for atoms
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
: If
[x]\subseteq\{e_{0},e_{1}\}
, then
\pi(\ell(\mathbf{g}_{[x]}))=\pi(e)
, and
g_{[x]}(\pi)([x])=\pi[x]\subseteq\{\pi(e_{0}),\pi(e_{1})\}
, so
\ell(\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{[x]}))=\pi(e)
.

The fact that
\sigma\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(V_{C})
follows because for each orbit
\Omega_{[x]}
, we have that
g_{[x]}(\pi)\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{\Omega_{[x]}})
, and the set of gates
V_{C}
can be partitioned into these orbits so that each part has the form
\{\mathbf{g}_{i,[x^{\prime}],[y]}\mid(i,[x^{\prime}],[y])\in I_{\Omega_{[x]}}\}
, for some orbit
\Omega_{[x]}
.

It remains to prove that
\sigma
preserves the wire structure of the circuit. For any two gates
\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]},\mathbf{g}_{j,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]}
, we have

(\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]},\mathbf{g}_{j,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]})\in E_{C}\text{ if and only if }[y]=[x^{\prime}]\text{ and }(N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),(j,[y],[y^{\prime}])}=1.

The latter equation holds if and only if

(N\pi[x]\pi[y])_{h_{[x]}(\pi)(i,[x],[y]),g_{[y]}(\pi)(j,[y],[y^{\prime}])}=1.

This is true by Lemma
45
. If
[x^{\prime}]=[y]
, then we have
\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{j,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]})=\mathbf{g}_{g_{[y]}(\pi)(j,[y],[y^{\prime}])}
. Furthermore, it holds
\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]})=\mathbf{g}_{g_{[x]}(\pi)(i,[x],[y])}
. By the definition in the previous section,
g_{[x]}=h_{[x]}
, and it holds that
g_{[x]}(\pi)(i,[x],[y])
is of the form
(\ell,\pi[x],\pi[y])
, and
g_{[y]}(\pi)(j,[y],[y^{\prime}])
is of the form
(\ell^{\prime},\pi[y],\pi[y^{\prime}])
, so these are indeed row- and column-indices of the matrix
N\pi[x]\pi[y]
. So altogether, the above equation is equivalent to:

(\sigma\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]},\sigma\mathbf{g}_{j,[x^{\prime}],[y^{\prime}]})\in E_{C}.

∎

As in the case of the circuit
C(\mu)
, this lemma implies that the orbit-size of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
cannot be greater than the orbit-size of
\mu
(here,
\mathbf{Orb}_{G}(\mu)
again refers to the
\mathbf{Aut}(G)
-orbit and not to the
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}(\mu)
-orbit, which would be pointless).

Corollary 49
.

|\mathbf{Orb}_{G}(\widehat{C}(\mu))|\leq|\mathbf{Orb}_{G}(\mu)|.

Proof.

Analogous to the proof of Corollary
24
.
∎

Lemma 50
.

For every internal gate
\mathbf{g}:=\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}\in V_{C}
it holds:

{\cal X}(\mathbf{g})=\chi^{-1}(M[x]_{(i,[x],[y]),-}^{T}).

Proof.

By induction. For each input gate
\mathbf{g}_{[x]}
with
[x]\subseteq\{e_{0},e_{1}\}
we have
{\cal X}(\mathbf{g}_{[x]})=\{e\}
.

Now let
\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}
be an internal gate. By definition of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
, it holds

\mathbf{g}E_{C}=\{\mathbf{g}_{j,[y],[z]}\mid(j,[y],[z])\in I_{[y]},(N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),(j,[y],[z])}=1\}.

Thus,

\displaystyle{\cal X}(\mathbf{g})

\displaystyle=\bigtriangleup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(j,[y],[z])\in I_{[y]},}}{{(N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),(j,[y],[z])}=1}}}{\cal X}(\mathbf{g}_{j,[y],[z]})

\displaystyle=\chi^{-1}\Big(\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(j,[y],[z])\in I_{[y]},}}{{(N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),(j,[y],[z])}=1}}}\chi({\cal X}(\mathbf{g}_{j,[y],[z]}))\ \ \mod 2\Big)

\displaystyle=\chi^{-1}(((N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),-}\cdot M[y])^{T}).

The last step uses the induction hypothesis and the fact that
I_{[y]}
is the row-index-set of
M[y]
. By the definition of the matrix
M[x]
in the previous subsection, we have
(N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),-}\cdot M[y]=M[x]_{(i,[x],[y]),-}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 51
.

The
fan-in dimension
of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
is at most
\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu))
.

Proof.

Consider any gate
\mathbf{g}:=\mathbf{g}_{i,[x],[y]}\in V_{C}
. By definition of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
, the children of
\mathbf{g}
are

\mathbf{g}E_{C}=\{\mathbf{g}_{j,[y],[z]}\mid(j,[y],[z])\in I_{[y]},(N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),(j,[y],[z])}=1\}.

By Lemma
50
, we have for each of these children:

{\cal X}(\mathbf{g}_{j,[y],[z]})=\chi^{-1}(M[y]_{(j,[y],[z]),-}).

Thus, the gate matrix
M\mathbf{g}\in\bbF_{2}^{\mathbf{g}E_{C}\times E}
is a submatrix of
M[y]
: It consists precisely of those rows
(j,[y],[z])
of
M[y]
such that
(N[x][y])_{(i,[x],[y]),(j,[y],[z])}=1
. Therefore,

\textbf{rk}(M\mathbf{g})\leq\textbf{rk}(M[y]).

Now we can argue as in the proof of Lemma
27
in order to bound
\textbf{rk}(M[y])
. Using the Orbit-Stabiliser Theorem, we obtain again:

\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu))\geq|E|-\dim\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y).

By Lemma
46
, we have
\textbf{Ker}(M[y])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y)
. Therefore, with the Rank Theorem we obtain:

\textbf{rk}(M[y])=|E|-\dim\ \mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y)\leq\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu)).

In total, we have
\textbf{rk}(M\mathbf{g})\leq\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu))
. Since
\mathbf{g}\in V_{C}
was arbitrary, this is a bound on the fan-in dimension of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
.
∎

Lemma 52
.

For the root
r
of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
, it holds

|{\cal X}(r)|\geq\frac{|\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)|}{\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu))}.

Proof.

By definition of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
, we have
r=\mathbf{g}_{i,[\mu],[y]}
for some
(i,[\mu],[y])\in I_{[\mu]}
such that
M[\mu]_{(i,[\mu],[y]),-}
is a row with a maximum number of
1
-entries. We have

{\cal X}(r)=\chi^{-1}(M[\mu]_{(i,[\mu],[y]),-}^{T})

according to Lemma
50
. Therefore, we have to show that
M[\mu]
has a row with at least
\frac{|\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)|}{\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu))}
many
1
-entries.

Claim:
For every
e\in\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)
there is a row of
M[\mu]
which is non-zero in column
e
.

Proof of claim:
Since
\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)
is the minimal CFI-support of
\mu
, it holds that
\chi(e)\notin\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(\mu)
. Otherwise there is a smaller support not containing
e
. Suppose for a contradiction that all rows of
M[\mu]
are zero in column
e
. Then
\chi(e)\in\textbf{Ker}(M[\mu])
. But this contradicts the fact that
\textbf{Ker}(M[\mu])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(\mu)
(Lemma
46
). This proves the claim.

Now take a subset
{\cal B}
of the rows of
M[\mu]
that forms a basis of the row space of
M[\mu]
. By Lemma
51
, we have
|{\cal B}|\leq\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu))
. For every
e\in E
such that some row of
M[\mu]
is non-zero in column
e
, there must also be a row in
{\cal B}
that is non-zero in column
e
(else
{\cal B}
does not generate the whole row space). So by the claim and by the size bound on
{\cal B}
, there is a row in
{\cal B}
with at least
\frac{|\sup_{\text{CFI}}(\mu)|}{\log(\mathbf{maxOrb}_{E}(\mu))}
many
1
-entries.
∎

So far we have shown
Properties

1
,
2
and
3
from Theorem
32
.
Property 1
is proved by Corollary
49
,
Property 2
by Lemma
52
, and
Property 3
by Lemma
51
.
It remains to estimate the size of the circuit under the assumption that every space
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
has a symmetric basis.

6.3
Bounding the size of the circuit

Lemma 53
.

Let
\text{Atoms}(\mu)\subseteq\text{tc}(\mu)
denote the set of atoms in
\text{tc}(\mu)
, and
\text{Sets}(\mu):=\text{tc}(\mu)\setminus\text{Atoms}(\mu)
.
The size of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
is

|V_{C}|=|\{e\in E\mid e_{0}\text{ or }e_{1}\in\text{Atoms}(\mu)\}|+\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle([x],[y]),x\in\text{Sets}(\mu),}}{{[y]\in{\cal C}[x]}}}|J_{[x][y]}|.

In other words: The size of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
is determined by the total number of rows of all
N[x][y]
-matrices.

Proof.

By definition of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
,
|V_{C}|=\sum_{[x],x\in\text{tc}(\mu)}|I_{[x]}|
. For each
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)\cap\text{Sets}(\mu)
, we have
|I_{[x]}|=\sum_{[y]\in{\cal C}[x]}|J_{[x][y]}|
. Every set
J_{[x][y]}
is exclusively associated with the pair
([x],[y])
, so

\sum_{[x],x\in\text{tc}(\mu)}|I_{[x]}|=\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle([x],[y]),x\in\text{Sets}(\mu),}}{{[y]\in{\cal C}[x]}}}|J_{[x][y]}|.

For each atomic
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
,
|I_{[x]}|=1
.
∎

Hence, in order to bound
|V_{C}|
, we have to bound the number of rows of each of the
N[x][y]
-matrices. To do so, we revisit the proofs of Lemmas
39
and
41
. We will see that the matrix that is constructed in these lemmas can be chosen to have polynomial size if a symmetric basis for
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
exists. The first step in the construction of
N[x][y]
in Lemma
39
is to solve a family of linear equation systems. We now show that the symmetries of such systems correspond to symmetries of their solutions.

Lemma 54
.

Let
I,J
be abstract index sets and
A,A^{\prime}\in\bbF_{2}^{I\times J},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\in\bbF_{2}^{I}
such that the linear equation systems
A\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
and
A^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
each have a unique solution
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s^{\prime}}
, respectively. Let
\mathbf{G}\leq{\mathbf{Sym}}(J)
be a permutation group acting on the columns of
A
. Let
\pi\in\mathbf{G}
a permutation that maps the row-set of the extended coefficient matrix
(A|\mathbf{b})

R=\{(A_{i-},\mathbf{b}_{i})\mid i\in I\}

to the row-set
R^{\prime}
of
(A^{\prime}|\mathbf{b}^{\prime})
. Here, the action of
\pi
on
R
is
\pi(A_{i-},\mathbf{b}_{i}):=(\pi(A_{i-}),\mathbf{b}_{i})
. Then
\pi(\mathbf{s})=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We only have to show that
\pi(\mathbf{s})
is a solution of
A^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
. Then we have
\pi(\mathbf{s})=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
by uniqueness of the solution.
If
\pi\in\mathbf{G}
maps
R
to
R^{\prime}
, then there is a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I)
that is induced by the action of
\pi
, i.e.
\pi(A_{i-},\mathbf{b}_{i})=(A^{\prime}_{\sigma i,-},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{\sigma i})
for all
i\in I
. For every
i\in I
, it holds
A_{i-}\cdot\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
, because
\mathbf{s}
is a solution to the equation system. Since (by Proposition
34
)
A_{i-}\cdot\mathbf{s}=\pi(A_{i-})\cdot\pi(\mathbf{s})=A^{\prime}_{\sigma i-}\cdot\pi(\mathbf{s})
, and
\mathbf{b}_{i}=\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{\sigma i}
,
\pi(\mathbf{s})
is a solution to the equation
A^{\prime}_{\sigma i-}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{\sigma i}
. Since every row of
(A^{\prime}|\mathbf{b}^{\prime})
has such a preimage under
\pi
, it follows that
\pi(\mathbf{s})
is a solution for every equation in
A^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
. By assumption, the equation system has a unique solution. Therefore,
\pi(\mathbf{s})=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
.
∎

The next lemma will become interesting once we are dealing with symmetric bases of vector spaces. It shows that the permutation invariance of a set of vectors (for example a symmetric basis) is preserved under linear maps and appropriate group homomorphisms.

Lemma 55
.

Let
M\in\bbF_{2}^{I\times J}
and let
\mathbf{G}\leq{\mathbf{Sym}}(J)
be a permutation group acting on the columns of
M
. Let
B\subseteq\bbF_{2}^{J}
be a set of vectors and
S:=\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{G}}(B)
. Let
g:\mathbf{G}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(I)
be a group homomorphism such that for all
\pi\in\mathbf{G}
,
(g(\pi),\pi)M=M
.

Then
g(S)
stabilises the set
M\cdot B=\{M\cdot\mathbf{v}\mid\mathbf{v}\in B\}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi\in S
and
\mathbf{v}\in B
. Let
\mathbf{w}=M\cdot\mathbf{v}
. We show that
(g(\pi))^{-1}(\mathbf{w})=M\cdot\pi(\mathbf{v})
. For each
i\in I
, we have

M_{i,-}\cdot\pi(\mathbf{v})=\pi(M_{(g(\pi))(i),-})\cdot\pi(\mathbf{v})=M_{(g(\pi))(i),-}\cdot\mathbf{v}.

The first equality holds because
(g(\pi),\pi)M=M
by assumption and the second one is due to Proposition
34
. It follows that
g(\pi)(M\cdot\pi(\mathbf{v}))=M\cdot\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{w}
. So
(g(\pi))^{-1}(\mathbf{w})=M\cdot\pi(\mathbf{v})
. We have
\pi(\mathbf{v})\in B
because
\pi\in S
. Therefore,
(g(\pi))^{-1}(\mathbf{w})\in M\cdot B
. Because
\mathbf{w}=M\cdot\mathbf{v}
was arbitrary, we know that
(g(\pi))^{-1}=g(\pi^{-1})\in g(S)
stabilises the set
M\cdot B
. Since
\pi\in S
was also arbitrary,
g(S)
stabilises
M\cdot B
.
∎

Finally, we provide the exact definition of what we mean by a
symmetric basis
. This definition is tailored to the spaces
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
that occur for the objects in
\text{tc}(\mu)
. When we say “symmetric basis”, we actually mean two bases: We require that both the basis of
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
as well as its extension to a basis of the ambient space be symmetric. Symmetry is meant in the sense that the orbit must have polynomial size.

Definition 56
.

Let
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
and
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
. We say that the vector space
\Gamma:=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)\leq\bbF_{2}^{E}
has a
symmetric basis
if there exist two bases

{\cal B}_{\Gamma}\subseteq{\cal B}

such that
{\cal B}_{\Gamma}
is a basis of
\Gamma=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
and
{\cal B}
is a basis of
\bbF_{2}^{E}
, and such that: The group

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}

\displaystyle({\cal B}_{\Gamma})\cap\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B})=

\displaystyle\{\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)\mid\pi({\cal B}_{\Gamma})={\cal B}_{\Gamma}\text{ and }\pi({\cal B})={\cal B}\}\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)

has index
\leq\text{poly}(|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|)
in
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
.

In the above definition, the polynomial
\text{poly}(\cdot)
is of course meant to be fixed for the whole family of CFI-instances that we are considering in Theorem
32
.
Now let us continue with the main lemma that bounds the size of
N[x][y]
assuming the existence of a symmetric basis.

Lemma 57
.

Let
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
and
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
. If
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
has a
symmetric basis
, then the number of rows of
N[x][y]
is polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|
.

Proof.

We show that there is a Boolean matrix
N
with a polynomial number of rows which satisfies conditions (i) and (ii) from Lemma
41
. This proves the lemma because
N[x][y]
is defined as the smallest such matrix.

Consider the proof of Lemma
39
. When applied to
\Gamma=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
and
\Delta=M[y]\cdot\bbF_{2}^{E}=\textbf{Im}(M[y])
, the proof shows that there is a matrix
N\in\bbF_{2}^{[k]\times I_{[y]}}
, for
k=\dim(\textbf{Im}\ M[y])-\dim(M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x))
, such that
\textbf{Ker}(N)\cap\textbf{Im}(M[y])=M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. In the proof,
k
linear equation systems
A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
are defined, each one with a unique solution. Then, for
i\in[k]
, the
i
-th row of
N
is defined as the unique solution to the equation system
A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
. The matrix
A_{i}
is the same for every
i\in[k]
.
It depends on the choice of a basis for
M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
and an extension to a basis of
\textbf{Im}(M[y])
. More precisely, let
\{\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{k}\}
be the vectors that extend the basis of
M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
to a basis of
\textbf{Im}(M[y])
.
The rows of
A_{i}
are the basis vectors of
\textbf{Im}(M[y])
, and the vector
\mathbf{b}_{i}
has exactly one
1
-entry in the row corresponding to
\mathbf{w}_{i}
. In this way,
\mathbf{b}_{i}
is defined for every
i\in[k]
.

One can check that this proof of Lemma
39
still goes through if one uses a
generating set
for the space
M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
instead of a basis for the rows of
A_{i}
– as long as the extension
\{\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{k}\}
to a basis of the full space
\textbf{Im}(M[y])
is a linearly independent set of vectors. This changes nothing and in particular, each equation system
A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}
will still have a unique solution because we have just added some redundant equations.

We choose appropriate bases now. Since
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
has a symmetric basis by assumption, there are bases
{\cal B}_{\Gamma}\subseteq{\cal B}
of
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
and
\bbF_{2}^{E}
, respectively, such that
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}_{\Gamma})\cap\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B})
has small index in
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
. Let
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}:=M[y]\cdot{\cal B}_{\Gamma}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
and
{\cal B}^{\prime}:=M[y]\cdot{\cal B}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. Then
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
is a generating set for
M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
, and
{\cal B}^{\prime}\setminus{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
extends this generating set to a basis of
\textbf{Im}(M[y])
. Importantly,
{\cal B}^{\prime}\setminus{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
is a linearly independent set of vectors (while
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
may be linearly dependent). This is because
\textbf{Ker}(M[y])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y)
(Lemma
46
), and
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y)\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
. Thus, if there were a subset
K\subseteq{\cal B}^{\prime}\setminus{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
such that
\sum K=0
, then the sum of the
M[y]
-preimages of the vectors in
K
would be in
\textbf{Ker}(M[y])=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y)
. This cannot be the case because
{\cal B}_{\Gamma}
is a basis for
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)\geq\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y)
, so no linear combination of vectors in
{\cal B}\setminus{\cal B}_{\Gamma}
can be in
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}(y)
.

Now apply Lemma
55
to the matrix
M=M[y]\in\bbF_{2}^{I_{[y]}\times E}
,
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x),B={\cal B}_{\Gamma}
and the homomorphism
g
defined like this:
g:\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
maps any
\pi\in\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
to the
I_{[y]}
-restriction of the permutation
g_{[y]}(\pi)\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{\Omega_{[y]}})
. This is well-defined and
g(\pi)\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
because
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)\leq\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y])
by Corollary
36
, and
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]))
maps
I_{[y]}
to itself by Lemma
47
.

Now it follows with Lemma
55
that
g(\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}_{\Gamma}))\leq{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
stabilises the set
M[y]\cdot{\cal B}_{\Gamma}\subseteq\bbF_{2}^{I_{[y]}}
. It holds
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}=M[y]\cdot{\cal B}_{\Gamma}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, and the zero-vector forms a singleton orbit with respect to permutations of the entries, so also
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
is stabilised by
g(\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}_{\Gamma}))
.
Similarly, by applying Lemma
55
to
B={\cal B}
, we get that
g(\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}))
stabilises
{\cal B}^{\prime}
.

Every permutation
\pi\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
that stabilises the sets
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
and
{\cal B}^{\prime}
induces a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathbf{Sym}}({\cal B}^{\prime})
, whose restriction to
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
is a permutation in
{\mathbf{Sym}}({\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma})
. Since the rows of each coefficient matrix
A_{i}
are the vectors in
{\cal B}^{\prime}
, every
\pi\in{\mathbf{Sym}}(I_{[y]})
that fixes
{\cal B}^{\prime}
setwise reorders the rows of
A_{i}
. The right hand side
\mathbf{b}_{i}
has only one
1
-entry in the row corresponding to
\mathbf{w}_{i}\in{\cal B}^{\prime}\setminus{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
. So if
\pi
also stabilises
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
, the action of
\pi
on
(A_{i}|\mathbf{b}_{i})
moves the row vector
\pi^{-1}(\mathbf{w}_{i})\in{\cal B}^{\prime}\setminus{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
to the row where
\mathbf{b}_{i}
has its
1
-entry. Up to a reordering of rows, this yields one of the other linear equation systems
\{(A_{i}|\mathbf{b}_{i})\mid i\in[k]\}
, because in some linear equation system, the equation with coefficient vector
\pi^{-1}(\mathbf{w}_{i})
has a
1
on the right hand side. So any
\pi
that stabilises both
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
and
{\cal B}^{\prime}
induces a permutation on the set of linear equation systems
\{(A_{i}|\mathbf{b}_{i})\mid i\in[k]\}
in the sense of Lemma
54
(with the action of column permutations on the row set of an equation system as defined there).
Let

{\cal S}:=g(\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}_{\Gamma}))\cap g(\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B})).

As we argued above, this group fixes both
{\cal B}^{\prime}
and
{\cal B}^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
.
Therefore, it induces a permutation on the equation systems and so Lemma
54
tells us that
{\cal S}
also induces a corresponding permutation on the set

\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\bbF_{2}^{I_{[y]}}\mid\mathbf{s}_{i}\text{ is the unique solution to }A_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}_{i}\}

(where
{\cal S}
acts on these vectors by permuting the entries). These solution vectors form exactly the rows of the matrix
N\in\bbF_{2}^{[k]\times I_{[y]}}
that is being constructed in the proof of Lemma
39
. Therefore, the group
{\cal S}
acting on the columns of
N
induces corresponding permutations on the rows of
N
. In other words, for every
\pi\in{\cal S}
there is a
\sigma\in{\mathbf{Sym}}_{k}
such that
(\sigma,\pi)N=N
. Now we close the rows of
N
under the action of
g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
, exactly like in the proof of Lemma
41
. This yields a matrix satisfying the desired conditions (a) and (b). We now argue that for each row of
N
, only
\text{poly}(k+|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|)
many rows are added to form the closure under
g_{[y]}(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
.

To show this, we have to bound the size of

\mathbf{Orb}(N_{i-})=\{\pi(N_{i-})\mid\pi\in g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))\}
. Let
\mathbf{Orb}(N)
denote the
g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
-orbit of the set of rows of
N
, and
\mathbf{Stab}(N)\leq g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
the setwise stabiliser of the set of rows. It holds
|\mathbf{Orb}(N_{i-})|\leq k\cdot|\mathbf{Orb}(N)|
because any image of the row
N_{i-}
is an element of at least one of the
k
-element row sets in
\mathbf{Orb}(N)
. Together with the Orbit-Stabiliser Theorem, we get

|\mathbf{Orb}(N_{i-})|\leq k\cdot|\mathbf{Orb}(N)|=k\cdot\frac{|g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))|}{|\mathbf{Stab}(N)|}.

By what we argued above, we have

{\cal S}\leq\mathbf{Stab}(N)
and thus

\displaystyle\frac{|g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))|}{|\mathbf{Stab}(N)|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))|}{|{\cal S}|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))|}{|g(\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}_{\Gamma})\cap\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}))|}.

The last inequality holds because
g(\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}_{\Gamma})\cap\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}))\leq{\cal S}
.
It is not difficult to prove that the application of a group homomorphism can only decrease the index of
H
in
G
, i.e.
[h(G):h(H)]\leq[G:H]
. According to Definition
56
, the index of
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}_{\Gamma})\cap\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B})
in
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
is polynomially bounded in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|
. As
g
is a group homomorphism, this is also a bound for the index of the image under
g
, which is equal to the fraction above.
In total, we have shown:

|\mathbf{Orb}(N_{i-})|\leq k\cdot\text{poly}(|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|)\leq\text{poly}(k+|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|).

Now this orbit bound applies to each of the
k
rows of
N
, so when closing the rows of
N
under the action of
g(\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x))
, we add at most
\text{poly}(k+|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|)
many new rows to the matrix. Because
k=\dim(\textbf{Im}\ M[y])-\dim(M[y]\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x))\leq\dim(\textbf{Im}\ M[y])\leq|E|
(this holds because
M[y]
is a linear map defined on the
|E|
-dimensional space
\bbF_{2}^{E}
),
\text{poly}(k+|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|)=\text{poly}(|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|)
. The resulting matrix is a candidate for
N[x][y]
, so this shows that
N[x][y]
has at most
\text{poly}(|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|)
many rows.
∎

Lemma 58
.

If for all
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
and all
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
,
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
has a symmetric basis, then
|V_{C}|
has size polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|
.

Proof.

This follows directly from Lemma
53
and Lemma
57
(remember that
|J_{[x][y]}|
is the number of rows of
N[x][y]
), and from the fact that
|\text{tc}(\mu)|
is polynomial in
|{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}|
because
\mu
is CPT-definable in
{\mathfrak{G}}^{S}
.
∎

This proves
Property 4
from Theorem
32
.

6.4
Which vector spaces have symmetric bases?

We have shown that the size of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
can be polynomially bounded if for all
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
and all
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
, the stabiliser space
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
has a
symmetric basis
. If this is not the case, then we do not know anything about the size of
\widehat{C}(\mu)
. There may be other ways to bound it but a priori we have to assume that it is super-polynomial then. This makes these symmetric XOR-circuits less useful for deriving lower bounds against CPT than in the CFI-symmetric case, unless, of course, we know that the object
\mu
satisfies the symmetric basis property.

This leads to the question what the class of h.f. sets with the symmetric basis property looks like. We do not have a definitive answer but we can show: All CFI-symmetric objects admit symmetric bases, there are easy examples of objects with symmetric bases which are not CFI-symmetric, and there also exist objects which do not admit symmetric bases but may a priori be CPT-definable. This latter result shows that there is unfortunately little hope to generally prove that
all
CPT-definable objects over CFI-graphs admit symmetric bases.

Lemma 59
.

Let
\mu\in\text{HF}(\widehat{E})
be CFI-symmetric. Then
\mu
satisfies the symmetric basis condition from Definition
56
.

Proof sketch.
Let
x\in\text{tc}(\mu)
and
[y]\in{\cal C}[x]
. Let
\Gamma:=\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)\leq\bbF_{2}^{E}
. We have to define two bases
{\cal B}_{\Gamma}\subseteq{\cal B}
of
\Gamma
and of
\bbF_{2}^{E}
, respectively, such that the group
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B}_{\Gamma})\cap\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)}({\cal B})
has polynomial index in
\mathbf{Stab}_{G}([y]\cap x)
. Since
\mu
is CFI-symmetric, by Definition
17
, the
\mathbf{Aut}_{\text{CFI}}({\mathfrak{G}})
-orbit of
[y]\cap x
has size exactly two. One can prove that then, the stabiliser space
\mathbf{Stab}_{E}([y]\cap x)
is a direct sum of a subspace on some coordinate set
I\subseteq E
containing exactly the vectors with even Hamming weight and the full Boolean space on coordinates
E\setminus I
. It is relatively easy to construct a basis for such a space whose orbit has only linear size: For the even subspace of
\bbF_{2}^{I}
one can fix one coordinate
i\in I
and take the basis
